Elliptical Regularized Hotelling Testing for High Dimensional Data
Long Feng, Le Zhou, Xiaoyi Wang, [5pt] School of Statistics, Data Science, Nankai University

Abstract
We consider one-sample testing of a high-dimensional location parameter under elliptically symmetric distributions with heavy tails and pervasive cross-sectional dependence. We propose an elliptical regularized Hotelling test with Cauchy combination (ERHT–CC), based on the sample spatial median and the spatial-sign covariance matrix centered at that median. We derive its null asymptotic normality, consistent estimators of the centering and variance, and an explicit local power function. Since the power-optimal ridge parameter depends on the unknown alternative, we aggregate fixed-ridge p p -values over a deterministic grid using the Cauchy rule. We establish a finite-grid joint Gaussian limit, justify the analytic combined p p -value without estimating cross-ridge correlations, and characterize its local power. Simulation studies and an empirical analysis demonstrate the favorable finite-sample performance of ERHT–CC under heavy tails and pervasive dependence.



Keywords:
Cauchy combination; elliptical distribution; high-dimensional location testing; regularized Hotelling statistic; spatial median; spatial-sign covariance matrix.

MSC 2020:
Primary 62H15; secondary 62H11, 62G35, 62E20.

1
Introduction

Testing a high-dimensional mean or location vector is a fundamental problem in modern multivariate analysis. It is encountered when a large collection of moment restrictions must be assessed jointly, as in asset-pricing models, portfolio evaluation, large cross-sectional panels, genomics, and imaging studies. The main statistical difficulties are now well understood: the dimension may be comparable to or larger than the sample size, the coordinates can be strongly dependent, and the observations can have heavy radial tails. These features make the classical Hotelling test unavailable or unstable and can invalidate procedures whose calibration relies strongly on empirical second and fourth moments. Under an elliptically symmetric distribution, the center remains a meaningful target even when ordinary moments are weak or nonexistent, so robust location testing provides a natural extension of high-dimensional mean testing. A broad overview of the latter literature is given by
Huang et al. 2022
.

One major line of research develops quadratic-form or sum-type tests that avoid inverting the sample covariance matrix. The trace-based approach dates back to
Dempster 1958
, and modern developments include
Bai and Saranadasa 1996
,
Srivastava and Du 2008
,
Srivastava 2009
,
Chen and Qin 2010
,
Park and Ayyala 2013
,
Srivastava et al. 2013
, and
Gregory et al. 2015
. Scale-standardized and bias-corrected extensions were developed for unequal marginal variances and unequal population covariance matrices; see, among others,
Feng and Sun 2015
,
Feng et al. 2015
, and
Feng et al. 2017
. These methods aggregate many coordinatewise contributions and are therefore particularly effective against dense alternatives. Their construction is also computationally attractive in large dimensions. Nevertheless, off-diagonal dependence is typically used only in the variance calibration, or is replaced by diagonal standardization. Consequently, the tests may fail to exploit favorable covariance directions when the dependence is strong, and procedures based directly on sample means and covariance moments may be sensitive to heavy-tailed observations.

A second line seeks to use the dependence structure more directly. Random-projection and random-subspace procedures first reduce the dimension and then apply a low-dimensional Hotelling statistic; representative examples are
Lopes et al. 2011
and
Thulin 2014
. Regularized Hotelling tests instead replace the unstable covariance inverse by a ridge inverse, as in
Chen et al. 2011
and
Li et al. 2020
. Precision-transformed and thresholded methods improve detection of sparse signals under dependence; see
Zhong et al. 2013
,
Cai et al. 2014
, and
Chen et al. 2019
. Factor-adjusted procedures remove a low-dimensional common component before testing, as in
Ma et al. 2015
and
He et al. 2020
. These approaches demonstrate that incorporating covariance geometry can substantially increase power. Their validity, however, generally requires a reliably estimable covariance, precision, or factor structure together with sufficiently regular moments. For ridge-based tests, the tuning parameter creates a further difficulty: its power-optimal value depends on both the population spectrum and the unknown orientation of the alternative, so a single data-independent choice need not perform uniformly well.

A related literature addresses adaptation to signal sparsity. Foundational detection-boundary results for sparse normal means and correlated signals motivate higher-criticism, maximum-type, and thresholding procedures; see
Donoho and Jin 2004
,
Hall and Jin 2010
, and
Arias-Castro et al. 2011
. In high-dimensional mean testing, multi-thresholding, sum–maximum combinations, and other adaptive constructions seek to bridge sparse and dense regimes; see
Zhong et al. 2013
,
Xu et al. 2016
,
Chen et al. 2019
, and the general developments reviewed by
Huang et al. 2022
. Robust versions based on ranks and spatial signs have also been proposed. In particular,
Zhang and Feng 2024
combine high-dimensional rank-based sum and maximum statistics, and
Liu et al. 2027
develop a spatial-sign max–sum test for location parameters. These methods reduce sensitivity to an unknown sparsity pattern, but their main objective is sparsity adaptation rather than covariance-aware normalization of dense signals under pervasive dependence.

Robust high-dimensional location testing has developed in parallel through spatial signs, spatial ranks, and inverse-distance weights. The spatial median and spatial-sign covariance matrix remove or strongly attenuate radial magnitudes while preserving multivariate directional information; see
Möttönen and Oja 1995
,
Visuri et al. 2000
, and
Oja 2010
. High-dimensional sign asymptotics and systematic comparisons among mean-, sign-, and rank-based tests were developed by
Paindaveine and Verdebout 2016
and
Chakraborty and Chaudhuri 2017
. For high-dimensional mean and location problems,
Wang et al. 2015
proposed a nonparametric spatial-sign test, and
Feng and Sun 2016
developed a scalar-invariant one-sample spatial-sign procedure under elliptical distributions. The effect of estimating location in two-sample sign procedures was treated through leave-one-out constructions by
Feng et al. 2016
, while
Feng et al. 2020
developed a high-dimensional spatial-rank test. Inverse-norm weighting was used by
Feng et al. 2021
for the one-sample problem and by
Huang et al. 2023
for the two-sample problem. More recently,
Yan et al. 2025
studied joint robust estimation of location and scatter for high-dimensional elliptical data. This body of work establishes that directional procedures can retain useful efficiency while being considerably less sensitive to heavy radial tails.

Important limitations nevertheless remain. Most existing robust location tests use scalar or diagonal standardization, or aggregate pairwise directional inner products without applying a regularized inverse of a full robust scatter matrix. They therefore do not fully exploit the dependence geometry that motivates Hotelling-type methods. Moreover, conventional normal calibrations for sum-type spatial-sign statistics are commonly derived under conditions that prevent a small number of eigen-directions from dominating. Such conditions exclude pervasive dependence structures, including compound symmetry with a fixed nonzero correlation. The recent analysis of
Zhao and Feng 2026
shows that the one-sample spatial-sign statistic of
Wang et al. 2015
can have a non-Gaussian limiting component when leading eigenvalues remain influential, which makes the strong-dependence issue substantive rather than technical.

To address these limitations, this paper develops a robust regularized Hotelling procedure. The proposed statistic combines the sample spatial median with the spatial-sign covariance matrix centered at that median and applies a ridge inverse to the latter. The shape matrix is allowed to contain a fixed number of pervasive eigenvalues proportional to the dimension, while the remaining spectrum forms a bounded bulk. This formulation includes fixed-correlation compound symmetry and permits a diverging condition number. The use of spatial quantities protects the procedure from large radial observations, whereas the regularized inverse retains covariance-direction information that is absent from purely scalar- or diagonal-standardized sign tests.

The paper makes four main contributions. First, it derives the null asymptotic normal distribution of the robust regularized Hotelling statistic when the dimension and sample size increase proportionally under a genuine elliptical model with pervasive dependence. The analysis explicitly retains the angular dependence of inverse distances and the first-order effect of centering the spatial-sign covariance matrix at the sample spatial median. Fully data-driven estimators of the asymptotic centering and variance are constructed and shown to be consistent. Second, the paper obtains a local power function for dense alternatives and identifies the signal-to-noise criterion that determines the oracle ridge parameter. Third, because this oracle parameter depends on the unknown alternative, the ridge-specific tests are evaluated over a fixed deterministic grid and combined by the Cauchy rule of
Liu and Xie 2020
. A finite-grid joint limit and the validity of the analytic combined p-value are established without estimating cross-ridge correlations. Fourth, the theory accommodates pervasive spikes rather than treating strong common dependence as a negligible perturbation.

The main technical contribution is a proof strategy tailored to the simultaneous presence of an estimated spatial center, heavy-tailed elliptical radii, and pervasive eigenvalues. Under pervasive dependence, the angular denominators do not concentrate at a constant, and the perturbation created by centering the spatial-sign covariance matrix at the sample spatial median is not negligible in operator norm. We derive a quadratic-form-accurate expansion of the spatial median, isolate the estimated-center effect into a rank-two correction and factor-induced finite-rank terms, and show that ridge resolvents suppress the latter along the pervasive eigenspace. This reduces the statistic to weighted companion-resolvent functionals for a separable random matrix with random column scales. We then combine deterministic equivalents for these functionals with a conditional Rademacher quadratic-form central limit theorem. The resulting hybrid argument yields the joint fixed-ridge Gaussian limit and the
O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1})
accuracy required for feasible centering; neither a standard spatial-median Bahadur expansion nor a classical linear spectral-statistic central limit theorem is sufficient for these conclusions.

The Monte Carlo study indicates that the proposed procedure can improve on several existing dense high-dimensional tests, especially when heavy tails or pervasive dependence weaken covariance-based or diagonally standardized competitors. An analysis of paired lung-cancer gene-expression data further illustrates the behavior of the method in an application with many correlated coordinates.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
presents the elliptical model, the statistic, its null calibration, and the local power analysis. Section
3
develops fixed-grid Cauchy aggregation and its joint asymptotic theory. Section
4
reports the simulation results, Section
5
presents the empirical application, and Section
6
concludes. Auxiliary results and detailed proofs are provided in Appendices
A
–
H
.

2
Methodology and asymptotic theory

Notation.

For a vector,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
denotes the Euclidean norm. For a matrix,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}
,
\operatorname{tr}(\cdot)
, and
\operatorname{rank}(\cdot)
denote the operator norm, Frobenius norm, trace, and rank, respectively. The symbols
\lambda_{\min}(\cdot)
and
\lambda_{\max}(\cdot)
denote the smallest and largest eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix, and
\delta_{x}
denotes the unit point mass at
x
. We write
O_{\mathbb{P}}(a_{n})
and
o_{\mathbb{P}}(a_{n})
for stochastic orders,
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
when their ratio is bounded above and away from zero, and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}
and
\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}
for convergence in distribution and probability, respectively. The symbol
\Phi
denotes the standard normal distribution function and
z_{q}=\Phi^{-1}(q)
its
q
th quantile.

2.1
Elliptical location model

Let
\bm{X}_{1},\ldots,\bm{X}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be independent observations with location parameter
\bm{\theta}_{p}
. We test

H_{0}:\bm{\theta}_{p}=\bm{\theta}_{0,p}\qquad\text{against}\qquad H_{1}:\bm{\theta}_{p}\neq\bm{\theta}_{0,p},

where
\bm{\theta}_{0,p}
is specified. We assume the elliptically symmetric representation

\bm{X}_{i}=\bm{\theta}_{p}+\sqrt{p}\,R_{i}\bm{\Omega}_{p}^{1/2}\frac{\bm{G}_{i}}{\left\lVert\bm{G}_{i}\right\rVert},\qquad\bm{G}_{i}\sim N(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}_{p}),

(2.1)

where
R_{i}>0
is independent of
\bm{G}_{i}
. The positive-definite shape matrix is normalized by
p^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}_{p})=1
. This normalization fixes the otherwise unidentified product of radial and shape scales.

For later use, define

\displaystyle h_{i,p}

\displaystyle=p^{-1}\bm{G}_{i}^{\top}\bm{G}_{i},

\displaystyle q_{i,p}

\displaystyle=p^{-1}\bm{G}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\Omega}_{p}\bm{G}_{i},

\displaystyle\ell_{i,p}

\displaystyle=(h_{i,p}/q_{i,p})^{1/2},

\displaystyle\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=R_{i}^{-1}.

The population spatial sign and the scaled inverse Euclidean distance are

\bm{U}_{i}=\frac{\bm{\Omega}_{p}^{1/2}\bm{G}_{i}}{(\bm{G}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\Omega}_{p}\bm{G}_{i})^{1/2}},\qquad w_{i}=\frac{\sqrt{p}}{\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta}_{p}\right\rVert}=\xi_{i}\ell_{i,p}.

(2.2)

Thus
\left\lVert\bm{U}_{i}\right\rVert=1
and

\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{\theta}_{p}=\sqrt{p}\,w_{i}^{-1}\bm{U}_{i}.

Although
\xi_{i}
is independent of
\bm{U}_{i}
, the Euclidean inverse-distance weight
w_{i}
is not, because
\ell_{i,p}
is angular.

2.2
Spatial estimators and regularized quadratic form

For
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, define
\mathbf{U}(\bm{x})=\bm{x}/\left\lVert\bm{x}\right\rVert
when
\bm{x}\neq\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{0}
. The sample spatial median and its centered spatial-sign covariance matrix are

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\theta}}

\displaystyle\in\arg\min_{\bm{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{t}\right\rVert,

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{p}\,\mathbf{U}(\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{i}\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{i}^{\top}.

We refer to
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{n}
as the centered spatial-sign covariance matrix (SSCM). Under the distributional conditions imposed below, the observations are not contained in an affine line with probability one for all sufficiently large
n
; hence the sample spatial median is unique almost surely. Its score equation gives
n^{-1}\sum_{i}\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
and
\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{n})=p
. For
\rho>0
, define

T_{n}(\rho)=n(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}_{0,p})^{\top}(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{n}+\rho\mathbf{I}_{p})^{-1}(\widehat{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}_{0,p}).

A deterministic location shift changes
\widehat{\bm{\theta}}
by the same shift and leaves
\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{n}
and all residual-based nuisance estimators unchanged; the test statistic itself additionally contains the resulting location-signal quadratic and cross terms.

2.3
Null distribution and feasible calibration

The following primitive conditions are imposed only for the distribution theory.

Assumption 1
.

The representation (
2.1
) holds and the following conditions are satisfied.

(i)

The pairs
(R_{i},\bm{G}_{i})
are independent and identically distributed across
i
. The variables
R_{i}
are independent of all Gaussian directions, their common distribution does not depend on
(n,p)
, and, with
\xi_{i}=R_{i}^{-1}
,

0<\xi_{i}\leq\bar{\xi}\quad\text{almost surely},\qquad m_{1}:=\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}\geq m_{-}>0.

Put
m_{k}=\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}^{k}
for
k=0,1,2
, with
m_{0}=1
.

(ii)

0<c_{-}\leq c_{n}:=p/n\leq c_{+}<\infty,\qquad c_{n}\to c\in(0,\infty),

and
\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
for fixed
0<\rho_{0}<\rho_{1}<\infty
.

(iii)

The deterministic shape matrix has the spectral decomposition

\bm{\Omega}_{p}=\mathbf{P}_{F,p}\bm{\Lambda}_{F,p}\mathbf{P}_{F,p}^{\top}+\mathbf{P}_{B,p}\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\mathbf{P}_{B,p}^{\top}.

Here
r\in\{0,1,\ldots\}
is fixed,
\mathbf{P}_{F,p}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}
and
\mathbf{P}_{B,p}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(p-r)}
have orthonormal columns spanning the pervasive and bulk eigenspaces, respectively. When
r=0
,
\mathbf{P}_{F,p}
is the empty
p\times 0
matrix,
\bm{\Pi}_{F,p}=\mathbf{0}
,
\mathbf{P}_{B,p}
is orthogonal, and every sum, block, and assertion indexed by a factor coordinate is understood to be vacuous. In all cases,

\bm{\Lambda}_{F,p}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{1,p},\ldots,\lambda_{r,p}),\qquad\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{r+1,p},\ldots,\lambda_{p,p}),

and

\bm{\Pi}_{F,p}=\mathbf{P}_{F,p}\mathbf{P}_{F,p}^{\top},\qquad\bm{\Pi}_{B,p}=\mathbf{P}_{B,p}\mathbf{P}_{B,p}^{\top}=\mathbf{I}_{p}-\bm{\Pi}_{F,p}

are the associated orthogonal projectors. The eigenvalues satisfy

0<\tau_{-}\leq\tau_{a,p}:=\lambda_{a,p}/p\leq\tau_{+}<1,\qquad 1\leq a\leq r,

and

0<\omega_{-}\leq\lambda_{j,p}\leq\omega_{+}<\infty,\qquad r<j\leq p.

Writing

\mu_{0,p}=p^{-1}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\lambda_{j,p}=1-\sum_{a=1}^{r}\tau_{a,p},

there is a constant
\mu_{-}>0
such that
\mu_{0,p}\geq\mu_{-}
.

(iv)

The empirical bulk spectral distribution

H_{B,p}=\frac{1}{p-r}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\delta_{\lambda_{j,p}}

converges weakly to a probability distribution
H_{B}
supported on
[\omega_{-},\omega_{+}]
, and
\tau_{a,p}\to\tau_{a}\in[\tau_{-},\tau_{+}]
.

Assumption
1
permits

\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Omega}_{p})/\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\Omega}_{p})=O(p)
and includes fixed-correlation compound symmetry. Since
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\Omega}_{p})\geq\omega_{-}
for all sufficiently large
p
, (
2.2
) implies

0<w_{i}\leq\bar{w}:=\bar{\xi}\,\omega_{-}^{-1/2}\qquad\text{almost surely}.

No positive moment of the upper radial tail
R_{i}
is required.

2.3.1
Theoretical null distribution

Under
H_{0}
, define

\bm{Y}_{i}=\sqrt{p}\,\bm{U}_{i},\qquad\mathbf{Y}=(\bm{Y}_{1},\ldots,\bm{Y}_{n}),\qquad\mathbf{B}_{n}=n^{-1}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{Y}^{\top},\qquad\mathbf{Q}_{n}=(\mathbf{B}_{n}+\rho\mathbf{I}_{p})^{-1}.

The companion matrix is

\mathbf{A}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{Y}^{\top}\mathbf{Q}_{n}\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{I}_{n}-\rho\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{Y}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}+\rho\mathbf{I}_{n}\right)^{-1}.

Put

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{n}),

\displaystyle e_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i},

\displaystyle t_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2},

\displaystyle b_{1,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{A}_{n})_{ii}w_{i},

\displaystyle b_{2,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{A}_{n})_{ii}w_{i}^{2}.

The conditional denominator and centering are

D_{n}=(e_{n}-b_{1,n})^{2}+\kappa_{n}(t_{n}-b_{2,n}),\qquad\mu_{n}=\frac{\kappa_{n}}{D_{n}}.

(2.3)

For
a,b\in\{0,1,2\}
, define the weighted off-diagonal companion functionals

\psi_{ab,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq j}(\mathbf{A}_{n})_{ij}^{2}w_{i}^{a}w_{j}^{b}.

They satisfy
\psi_{ab,n}=\psi_{ba,n}
. Let

\bm{\Gamma}_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}2\psi_{00,n}&2\psi_{01,n}&2\psi_{11,n}\\
2\psi_{01,n}&\psi_{02,n}+\psi_{11,n}&2\psi_{12,n}\\
2\psi_{11,n}&2\psi_{12,n}&2\psi_{22,n}\end{pmatrix}

(2.4)

and

\bm{g}_{n}=D_{n}^{-2}\begin{pmatrix}(e_{n}-b_{1,n})^{2}\\
2\kappa_{n}(e_{n}-b_{1,n})\\
\kappa_{n}^{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{D,n}^{2}=\bm{g}_{n}^{\top}\bm{\Gamma}_{n}\bm{g}_{n}.

(2.5)

For a nonzero vector
\bm{y}
, let
\mathfrak{o}(\bm{y})
be the unique member of
\{\bm{y},-\bm{y}\}
whose first nonzero coordinate is positive, and set

\mathcal{F}_{n}^{0}=\sigma\{w_{i},\mathfrak{o}(\bm{Y}_{i}):1\leq i\leq n\},

where
\sigma\{\cdot\}
denotes the sigma-field generated by its arguments. Conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n}^{0}
, the unrecorded global signs of the
\bm{Y}_{i}
are independent Rademacher variables, each taking the values
-1
and
1
with probability
1/2
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Under Assumption
1
and
H_{0}
, for every fixed
\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left|\mathbb{P}\left\{\left.\frac{T_{n}(\rho)-n\mu_{n}}{\sqrt{n\sigma_{D,n}^{2}}}\leq x\right|\mathcal{F}_{n}^{0}\right\}-\Phi(x)\right|\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0

(2.6)

Hence the same convergence holds unconditionally. Moreover, there are constants
0<c_{1}<c_{2}<\infty
such that

\mathbb{P}\{c_{1}\leq D_{n},\mu_{n},\sigma_{D,n}^{2}\leq c_{2}\}\to 1.

Consequently,
n\mu_{n}
and
n\sigma_{D,n}^{2}
are the conditional centering and variance scale in the Gaussian approximation, and both are of order
n
.

2.3.2
Feasible centering and variance

All quantities in (
2.3
)–(
2.5
) have direct sample analogues. Define

\widehat{w}_{i}=\frac{\sqrt{p}}{\left\lVert\bm{X}_{i}-\widehat{\bm{\theta}}\right\rVert},\qquad\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}_{n}=(\widehat{\mathbf{R}}_{n}+\rho\mathbf{I}_{p})^{-1},\qquad\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}^{\top}\widehat{\mathbf{Q}}_{n}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}},

where
\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}=(\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{n})
. Put

\displaystyle\widehat{\kappa}_{n}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{n}),

\displaystyle\widehat{e}_{n}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i}\widehat{w}_{i},

\displaystyle\widehat{t}_{n}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i}\widehat{w}_{i}^{2},

\displaystyle\widehat{b}_{1,n}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{n})_{ii}\widehat{w}_{i},

\displaystyle\widehat{b}_{2,n}

\displaystyle=n^{-1}\sum_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{n})_{ii}\widehat{w}_{i}^{2}.

Then

\widehat{D}_{n}=(\widehat{e}_{n}-\widehat{b}_{1,n})^{2}+\widehat{\kappa}_{n}(\widehat{t}_{n}-\widehat{b}_{2,n}),\qquad\widehat{\mu}_{n}=\frac{\widehat{\kappa}_{n}}{\widehat{D}_{n}}.

For
a,b\in\{0,1,2\}
, set

\widehat{\psi}_{ab,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq j}(\widehat{\mathbf{A}}_{n})_{ij}^{2}\widehat{w}_{i}^{a}\widehat{w}_{j}^{b},

form
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{n}
by replacing
\psi_{ab,n}
with
\widehat{\psi}_{ab,n}
in (
2.4
), and define

\widehat{\bm{g}}_{n}=\widehat{D}_{n}^{-2}\begin{pmatrix}(\widehat{e}_{n}-\widehat{b}_{1,n})^{2}\\
2\widehat{\kappa}_{n}(\widehat{e}_{n}-\widehat{b}_{1,n})\\
\widehat{\kappa}_{n}^{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\widehat{\sigma}_{D,n}^{2}=\widehat{\bm{g}}_{n}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}_{n}\widehat{\bm{g}}_{n}.

The fully feasible statistic is

Z_{n}(\rho)=\frac{T_{n}(\rho)-n\widehat{\mu}_{n}}{\sqrt{n\widehat{\sigma}_{D,n}^{2}}}.

Theorem 2.2
.

Under Assumption
1
and
H_{0}
, for every fixed
\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
,

Z_{n}(\rho)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}N(0,1).

(2.7)

More precisely,

\widehat{\mu}_{n}-\mu_{n}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1}),\qquad\widehat{\sigma}_{D,n}^{2}-\sigma_{D,n}^{2}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}),

and
\widehat{\sigma}_{D,n}^{2}
is bounded away from zero and infinity with probability tending to one.

According to Theorem
2.2
, we rejects the null hypothesis when
Z_{n}(\rho)>z_{1-\alpha}
.

2.4
Local alternatives, power, and the ridge parameter

Let

\mu_{0}=1-\sum_{a=1}^{r}\tau_{a}=\int t\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B}(t),\qquad D^{0}=\left(\mu_{0}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\tau_{a}G_{a}^{2}\right)^{-1},

where
G_{1},\ldots,G_{r}
are independent
N(0,1)
variables, and let
F_{D}
denote the distribution of
D^{0}
. For
\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
, let
(\delta_{\rho},\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho})
be the unique positive solution of

\displaystyle\delta_{\rho}

\displaystyle=c\int\frac{t}{\rho(1+\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}t)}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B}(t),

(2.8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d}{\rho(1+\delta_{\rho}d)}\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D}(d).

(2.9)

Define

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rho}(d)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{\rho}d}{1+\delta_{\rho}d},

\displaystyle\kappa_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\int\lambda_{\rho}(d)\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D}(d),

\displaystyle s_{a}

\displaystyle=\int d^{a/2}\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D}(d),

\displaystyle b_{a,\rho}

\displaystyle=\int d^{a/2}\lambda_{\rho}(d)\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D}(d),\qquad a=1,2,

and

\displaystyle u_{\rho}

\displaystyle=c\int\frac{t^{2}}{(1+\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}t)^{2}}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B}(t),

\displaystyle v_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}}{(1+\delta_{\rho}d)^{2}}\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D}(d),

\displaystyle a_{k,\rho}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{1+k/2}}{(1+\delta_{\rho}d)^{2}}\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D}(d),

\displaystyle k=0,1,2.

The deterministic denominator is

D_{\rho}=m_{1}^{2}(s_{1}-b_{1,\rho})^{2}+m_{2}\kappa_{\rho}(s_{2}-b_{2,\rho}).

(2.10)

For
a,b\in\{0,1,2\}
, put

\Psi_{ab}(\rho)=m_{a}m_{b}\frac{u_{\rho}a_{a,\rho}a_{b,\rho}}{\rho^{2}-u_{\rho}v_{\rho}},\qquad m_{0}=1.

(2.11)

Let
\bm{\Gamma}_{\rho}
be the matrix in (
2.4
) with
\psi_{ab,n}
replaced by
\Psi_{ab}(\rho)
, and define

\bm{g}_{\rho}=D_{\rho}^{-2}\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}^{2}(s_{1}-b_{1,\rho})^{2}\\
2\kappa_{\rho}m_{1}(s_{1}-b_{1,\rho})\\
\kappa_{\rho}^{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\sigma_{E}^{2}(\rho)=\bm{g}_{\rho}^{\top}\bm{\Gamma}_{\rho}\bm{g}_{\rho}.

(2.12)

Appendix
B
shows that
D_{n}\to D_{\rho}
and
\sigma_{D,n}^{2}\to\sigma_{E}^{2}(\rho)
in probability. The stability inequality
u_{\rho}v_{\rho}<\rho^{2}
makes (
2.11
) finite, and
\sigma_{E}^{2}(\rho)>0
.

2.4.1
Bulk local alternatives and power

Let
\bm{p}_{j,p}
be the eigenvector of
\bm{\Omega}_{p}
associated with the bulk eigenvalue
\lambda_{j,p}
,
j>r
. Consider deterministic
\bm{d}_{p}
such that

\bm{\Pi}_{F,p}\bm{d}_{p}=\mathbf{0},\qquad 0<d_{-}\leq\left\lVert\bm{d}_{p}\right\rVert\leq d_{+}<\infty,

and define

\mathcal{G}_{p}=\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}(\bm{p}_{j,p}^{\top}\bm{d}_{p})^{2}\delta_{\lambda_{j,p}}.

(2.13)

Assume
\mathcal{G}_{p}\Rightarrow\mathcal{G}
, where
\mathcal{G}
is a nonzero finite measure on
[\omega_{-},\omega_{+}]
. The local alternative is

H_{1,n}(\mathcal{G}):\qquad\bm{\theta}_{p}=\bm{\theta}_{0,p}+n^{-1/4}\bm{d}_{p}.

(2.14)

Define

q_{\rho}(\mathcal{G})=\frac{1}{\rho}\int\frac{1}{1+\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}t}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{G}(t),\qquad\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathcal{G})=\frac{q_{\rho}(\mathcal{G})}{\sigma_{E}(\rho)}.

(2.15)

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds. Under (
2.14
), for every fixed
\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
,

Z_{n}(\rho)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}N\{\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathcal{G}),1\}.

(2.16)

Consequently, the limiting power of the level-
\alpha
test is

\beta_{\alpha}(\rho;\mathcal{G})=1-\Phi\{z_{1-\alpha}-\Lambda_{\rho}(\mathcal{G})\}.

(2.17)

More generally, under

\bm{X}_{i}=\bm{\theta}_{0,p}+\bm{\Delta}_{p}+\sqrt{p}\,R_{i}\bm{\Omega}_{p}^{1/2}\frac{\bm{G}_{i}}{\left\lVert\bm{G}_{i}\right\rVert},

(2.18)

if

\sqrt{n}\left\{\left\lVert\bm{\Pi}_{B,p}\bm{\Delta}_{p}\right\rVert^{2}+p^{-1}\left\lVert\bm{\Pi}_{F,p}\bm{\Delta}_{p}\right\rVert^{2}\right\}\to\infty,

(2.19)

then
\mathbb{P}_{H_{1}}\{Z_{n}(\rho)>z_{1-\alpha}\}\to 1
.

For a diffuse signal with
\mathcal{G}=s^{2}H_{B}
,

q_{\rho}(\mathcal{G})=\frac{s^{2}}{\rho}\int\frac{1}{1+\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}t}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B}(t),

whereas concentration in a bulk eigenspace with eigenvalue
t_{0}
gives

q_{\rho}(s^{2}\delta_{t_{0}})=\frac{s^{2}}{\rho(1+\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}t_{0})}.

Corollary
A.2
gives
H_{B}=\delta_{1-\varrho}
and

D^{0}=(1-\varrho+\varrho G^{2})^{-1}
, so all integrals in
(
2.10
)–(
2.15
) reduce to one-dimensional Gaussian expectations.

2.4.2
Oracle choice of the ridge parameter

Since
\Phi
is increasing, an oracle ridge parameter for signal profile
\mathcal{G}
is

\rho_{\mathcal{G}}^{\star}\in\arg\max_{\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]}\frac{q_{\rho}(\mathcal{G})}{\sigma_{E}(\rho)}.

(2.20)

The objective is continuous and the compact interval ensures existence. Multiplying
\mathcal{G}
by a positive constant leaves the optimizer unchanged. At an interior maximizer,

2\frac{q_{\rho}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G})}{q_{\rho}(\mathcal{G})}=\frac{\{\sigma_{E}^{2}(\rho)\}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{E}^{2}(\rho)}.

The canonical derivatives solve

\begin{pmatrix}\rho&u_{\rho}\\
v_{\rho}&\rho\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{\rho}^{\prime}\\
\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=-\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{\rho}\\
\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}\end{pmatrix},

(2.21)

and

q_{\rho}^{\prime}(\mathcal{G})=-\frac{q_{\rho}(\mathcal{G})}{\rho}-\frac{\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}^{\prime}}{\rho}\int\frac{t}{(1+\widetilde{\delta}_{\rho}t)^{2}}\,\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{G}(t).

The remaining derivatives are finite one-dimensional integrals; explicit formulas are given in Lemma
G.4
in Appendix
G
. Thus (
2.20
) can be evaluated by a one-dimensional numerical search.

3
Cauchy aggregation over a fixed ridge grid

The power calculation in Section
2.4
identifies an oracle ridge value
\rho_{\mathcal{G}}^{\star}
, but the optimizer depends on the unknown signal measure
\mathcal{G}
. Estimating
\mathcal{G}
under a local alternative is difficult and can introduce a second tuning problem. We therefore replace selection of one ridge value by aggregation over a deterministic finite set

\mathcal{R}_{K}=\{\rho^{(1)},\ldots,\rho^{(K)}\}\subset[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}],\qquad\rho_{0}\leq\rho^{(1)}<\cdots<\rho^{(K)}\leq\rho_{1},

where
K
is fixed as
n,p\to\infty
. For each
\rho^{(k)}
, write

Z_{n,k}=Z_{n}(\rho^{(k)}),\qquad p_{n,k}=1-\Phi(Z_{n,k}),\qquad 1\leq k\leq K.

Let
\varpi_{1},\ldots,\varpi_{K}
be fixed positive weights satisfying
\sum_{k=1}^{K}\varpi_{k}=1
. The Cauchy statistic and its analytic combined
p
-value are

T_{\mathrm{CC},n}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\varpi_{k}\tan\!\left[\pi\left\{\frac{1}{2}-p_{n,k}\right\}\right],\qquad p_{\mathrm{CC},n}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\pi}\arctan(T_{\mathrm{CC},n}).

The implementation below uses equal weights
\varpi_{k}=K^{-1}
. We call the resulting procedure ERHT–CC.

The marginal limit in Theorem
2.2
is not sufficient to justify aggregation, because the statistics at different ridge values are computed from the same spatial median, signs, and inverse distances. Appendix
H
therefore develops the cross-ridge weighted companion functionals. For
\rho,\eta\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
, it defines a deterministic cross-covariance matrix
\bm{\Gamma}_{\rho,\eta}
and

r(\rho,\eta)=\frac{\bm{g}_{\rho}^{\top}\bm{\Gamma}_{\rho,\eta}\bm{g}_{\eta}}{\sigma_{E}(\rho)\sigma_{E}(\eta)}.

Put
\mathbf{C}_{K}=\{r(\rho^{(k)},\rho^{(\ell)})\}_{k,\ell=1}^{K}
. The matrix
\mathbf{C}_{K}
is positive semidefinite, has unit diagonal, and has nonnegative entries. Appendix
H
also defines its finite-
p
deterministic counterpart
\mathbf{C}_{K,p}^{\star}=\{r_{p,k\ell}^{\star}\}_{k,\ell=1}^{K}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Under Assumption
1
and
H_{0}
, for every fixed deterministic grid
\mathcal{R}_{K}
,

(Z_{n,1},\ldots,Z_{n,K})^{\top}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}N_{K}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{C}_{K}).

Theorem
3.1
also defines a theoretical fixed-level benchmark. Let
\bm{V}=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{K})^{\top}\sim N_{K}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{C}_{K})
and define

T_{\mathrm{CC},\infty}^{0}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\varpi_{k}\tan\!\left[\pi\{\Phi(V_{k})-1/2\}\right].

Let
c_{\alpha,K}
be the unique value satisfying

\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathrm{CC},\infty}^{0}>c_{\alpha,K})=\alpha
; its existence and uniqueness follow from Lemma
H.3
. This critical value is used only to characterize the oracle joint-limit benchmark. The implemented analytic Cauchy procedure uses the standard-Cauchy cutoff
\cot(\pi\alpha)
and requires no estimate of
\mathbf{C}_{K}
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
3.1
,

\mathbb{P}\{T_{\mathrm{CC},n}>c_{\alpha,K}\}\to\alpha.

For the analytic Cauchy
p
-value, at every fixed
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
,

\mathbb{P}(p_{\mathrm{CC},n}\leq\alpha)\to\mathbb{P}\{T_{\mathrm{CC},\infty}^{0}\geq\cot(\pi\alpha)\}.

Furthermore,

\lim_{\alpha\downarrow 0}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}(p_{\mathrm{CC},n}\leq\alpha)}{\alpha}=1.

Thus the oracle joint-limit benchmark is asymptotically exact at an ordinary fixed level, whereas the implemented analytic Cauchy
p
-value has the dependence-robust small-tail validity associated with the Cauchy combination principle.

The distinction in Theorem
3.2
is important. The critical value
c_{\alpha,K}
depends on the unknown matrix
\mathbf{C}_{K}
and is retained only as a theoretical benchmark. Except under special dependence structures, the analytic standard-Cauchy cutoff need not be exactly level
\alpha
for a fixed conventional value such as
0.05
. It is used because it is closed form and avoids estimating cross-ridge dependence; the Monte Carlo study evaluates its finite-sample behavior at the 5% level.

The same finite-grid argument gives an explicit power function. Put

\bm{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{G})=\{\Lambda_{\rho^{(1)}}(\mathcal{G}),\ldots,\Lambda_{\rho^{(K)}}(\mathcal{G})\}^{\top}

and, for
\bm{V}\sim N_{K}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{C}_{K})
, define

T_{\mathrm{CC},\infty}(\mathcal{G})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\varpi_{k}\tan\!\left[\pi\{\Phi(V_{k}+\Lambda_{\rho^{(k)}}(\mathcal{G}))-1/2\}\right].

Theorem 3.3
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds. Under the local alternative (
2.14
),

(Z_{n,1},\ldots,Z_{n,K})^{\top}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}N_{K}\{\bm{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{R}}(\mathcal{G}),\mathbf{C}_{K}\}.

Consequently, the limiting power of the analytic ERHT–CC test is

\beta_{\mathrm{CC},\alpha}^{\mathrm{A}}(\mathcal{G})=\mathbb{P}\{T_{\mathrm{CC},\infty}(\mathcal{G})\geq\cot(\pi\alpha)\},

and the limiting power of the oracle joint-limit benchmark is

\beta_{\mathrm{CC},\alpha}^{\mathrm{J}}(\mathcal{G})=\mathbb{P}\{T_{\mathrm{CC},\infty}(\mathcal{G})>c_{\alpha,K}\}.

Under the general shifted model (
2.18
), if (
2.19
) holds, then

p_{\mathrm{CC},n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}0,\qquad\mathbb{P}_{H_{1}}(p_{\mathrm{CC},n}\leq\alpha)\to 1.

4
Monte Carlo evidence

We compare four one-sample location tests. The fixed-ridge statistic
T_{n}(\rho)
is referred to as the elliptical regularized Hotelling (ERHT) statistic. The proposed ERHT–CC procedure combines its ten marginal
p
-values over
\mathcal{R}_{10}=\{0.1,0.2,\ldots,1.0\}
with equal Cauchy weights. CQ denotes the one-sample specialization of the diagonal-deleted quadratic
U
-statistic of
Chen and Qin 2010
. WPL denotes the spatial-sign test of
Wang et al. 2015
; following its numerical implementation, each coordinate is standardized by its sample standard deviation before the spatial signs are formed.

RHT–CC is included as a covariance-based ridge benchmark. For every
\rho\in\mathcal{R}_{10}
, we compute the regularized Hotelling statistic of
Chen et al. 2011
from the sample mean and ordinary sample covariance matrix, convert it to a marginal
p
-value using its random-matrix calibration, and aggregate the ten
p
-values by exactly the same equal-weight Cauchy rule used for ERHT–CC. Thus the comparison between ERHT–CC and RHT–CC isolates the effect of replacing the sample mean and covariance matrix by the sample spatial median and its centered spatial-sign covariance matrix; it is not driven by different ridge-selection rules.

All observations are generated from

\bm{X}_{i}=\bm{\theta}_{p}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,

where
\bm{\Omega}_{p}
is standardized by
\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Omega}_{p})=p
. We consider the bounded-spectrum AR(1) matrix and the pervasive compound-symmetry matrix

\Omega_{p,jk}=0.5^{|j-k|},\qquad\bm{\Omega}_{p}=0.5\mathbf{I}_{p}+0.5\mathbf{1}_{p}\mathbf{1}_{p}^{\top},

respectively. The latter has largest eigenvalue
1+0.5(p-1)
and therefore directly examines the strong-dependence regime in Assumption
1
.

Three centered elliptical error distributions are used:

Gaussian:

\displaystyle\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\sim N_{p}(\mathbf{0},\bm{\Omega}_{p}),

Multivariate t-distribution:

\displaystyle\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\sim t_{5,p}\!\left(\mathbf{0},\frac{3}{5}\bm{\Omega}_{p}\right),

normal mixture:

\displaystyle\bm{\varepsilon}_{i}\sim 0.8N_{p}\!\left(\mathbf{0},\frac{1}{2.6}\bm{\Omega}_{p}\right)+0.2N_{p}\!\left(\mathbf{0},\frac{9}{2.6}\bm{\Omega}_{p}\right).

For the multivariate
t
distribution, the second argument denotes its scale matrix, so its covariance is
\bm{\Omega}_{p}
. The normal mixture also has covariance
\bm{\Omega}_{p}
. Consequently, the three designs differ in radial-tail behavior while sharing the same covariance normalization.

For the size study,
H_{0}:\bm{\theta}_{p}=\mathbf{0}
,
n\in\{100,200\}
, and
p\in\{100,200,400\}
. Both ridge-based methods use
\mathcal{R}_{10}
, and ERHT–CC uses the Bartlett center correction
a=8n/p
implemented in the accompanying program. The nominal level is
0.05
, and every entry is based on 1,000 Monte Carlo replications.

For the power study, we fix
(n,p)=(100,200)
and draw a new location vector independently in each replication. Three signal designs are considered:

isotropic dense:

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{p}\sim N_{p}(\mathbf{0},c\mathbf{I}_{p}),

covariance-aligned dense:

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{p}\sim N_{p}(\mathbf{0},c\bm{\Omega}_{p}),

four-sparse:

\displaystyle\theta_{p,j}=c\xi_{j}\,1\{j\in\mathcal{S}_{p}\},\qquad|\mathcal{S}_{p}|=4,

where
\mathcal{S}_{p}
is sampled uniformly from all four-element subsets of
\{1,\ldots,p\}
and the nonzero signs
\xi_{j}
are independent Rademacher variables. For the two dense designs,

c\in\{0,0.00025,0.00050,\ldots,0.00250\},

whereas for the sparse design,

c\in\{0,0.05,0.10,\ldots,0.50\}.

The different grids account for the fact that
c
is a variance multiplier in the dense alternatives and a coordinate amplitude in the sparse alternative. Each power point is based on 2,000 replications; hence its Monte Carlo standard error is at most
\{0.25/2000\}^{1/2}=0.0112
.

Tables
1
and
2
show that ERHT–CC remains reasonably close to the 5% target across the three radial distributions, both dependence structures, and all six
(n,p)
combinations. CQ and WPL are mildly liberal in a number of settings, with the largest distortion occurring for WPL when
p/n
is large under AR(1). RHT–CC is also liberal under Gaussian sampling but becomes conservative under the two heavy-tailed distributions, particularly under compound symmetry. Since ERHT–CC and RHT–CC use the same ridge grid and aggregation rule, their different behavior under heavy tails reflects the spatial-median and spatial-sign construction rather than tuning-parameter selection.

Figures
1
and
2
report the power curves under AR(1) and compound-symmetry dependence, respectively. In each figure, the rows correspond to
N_{p}(\mathbf{0},\bm{\Omega}_{p})
,
t_{5,p}(\mathbf{0},3\bm{\Omega}_{p}/5)
, and the standardized normal mixture, while the columns correspond to the isotropic dense, covariance-aligned dense, and four-sparse alternatives.

Figure 1:
Empirical power under AR(1) dependence,
\Omega_{p,jk}=0.5^{|j-k|}
, with
(n,p)=(100,200)
.

Under AR(1), whose eigenvalues remain uniformly bounded, ERHT–CC and WPL generally lie in the same broad power range. Their differences are especially modest for covariance-aligned and sparse alternatives, although the ranking can depend on the radial distribution and signal strength. Under Gaussian sampling, RHT–CC is sometimes slightly more powerful, which is consistent with the efficiency of covariance-based regularization when second-moment modeling is correct. Once the errors are Multivariate
t_{5}
or normal mixtures, ERHT–CC is usually above RHT–CC and CQ, with a particularly visible gain for dense isotropic signals. The robust procedure therefore pays little under bounded-spectrum dependence while protecting power against radial heavy tails.

Figure 2:
Empirical power under compound-symmetry dependence,
\bm{\Omega}_{p}=0.5\mathbf{I}_{p}+0.5\mathbf{1}_{p}\mathbf{1}_{p}^{\top}
, with
(n,p)=(100,200)
.

The distinction is much sharper under compound symmetry. ERHT–CC and RHT–CC exploit the inverse angular-scatter structure and attain rapidly increasing power, whereas WPL can remain close to its null rejection probability for dense signals and increases much more slowly for sparse signals. Thus, when the largest eigenvalue is proportional to
p
, ERHT–CC is substantially more powerful than WPL over most of the transition region. The heavy-tailed rows also display the main robustness advantage: ERHT–CC uniformly dominates CQ and is appreciably above RHT–CC at moderate signal strengths for both dense alternatives and for the sparse alternative before the power curves reach one. Under Gaussian errors, RHT–CC can retain a small advantage in some panels, but this advantage disappears or reverses under Multivariate
t_{5}
and normal-mixture sampling.

Taken together, the power results agree with the theoretical motivation. Under weak spectral dependence, the proposed test is competitive with the existing spatial-sign procedure; under pervasive dependence, ridge normalization of the centered SSCM produces a clear gain. Replacing Euclidean magnitudes by the spatial median and spatial signs is most consequential under heavy tails, where ERHT–CC substantially improves on the covariance-based RHT–CC and the CQ quadratic test while maintaining the favorable dependence adaptation of a regularized Hotelling statistic.

5
Real Data Application

We illustrate the proposed procedure using the publicly available GSE19804 data set from the NCBI Gene Expression Omnibus
(
GEO accession GSE19804
).
The study profiles transcriptional changes in lung cancer among nonsmoking women in Taiwan. Expression was measured on the Affymetrix Human Genome U133 Plus 2.0 Array (GPL570) for 60 lung-tumor specimens and the corresponding 60 adjacent normal-tissue specimens, giving 60 matched pairs and 54,675 probe-level measurements per specimen.

Let
\bm{T}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\bm{N}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
denote the tumor and matched normal expression vectors for patient
i
, respectively, and define the paired difference

\bm{X}_{i}=\bm{T}_{i}-\bm{N}_{i},\qquad i=1,\ldots,60,\qquad p=54{,}675.

The resulting one-sample problem tests whether the multivariate location of the paired expression changes is zero. We apply CQ, WPL, RHT–CC, and ERHT–CC using the same implementations and, for the two ridge procedures, the same grid
\mathcal{R}_{10}
as in Section
4
.

To describe the marginal signal strength, for each probe
j
we compute the paired Student statistic

t_{j}=\frac{\overline{X}_{j}}{s_{j}/\sqrt{60}},

where
\overline{X}_{j}
and
s_{j}
are the sample mean and standard deviation of
X_{1j},\ldots,X_{60,j}
. Positive values indicate higher expression in the tumor tissue. Figure
3
shows a broad, asymmetric distribution with substantial mass in both tails, indicating that the full probe set contains many pronounced marginal changes.

Figure 3:
Histogram of the paired probe-level Student-t statistics for the 54,675 probes in GSE19804. The solid vertical line marks zero and the dashed lines mark the empirical 2.5% and 97.5% quantiles.

All four global procedures strongly reject the null hypothesis when the complete probe set is used. Table
3
reports the numerical results. The Cauchy-combined
p
-values reach the numerical reporting floor used in the implementation.

Table 3:
Global tests using all 54,675 probes in GSE19804.

-value

<10^{-15}

The overwhelming significance of the full analysis leaves little scope for distinguishing the four procedures. We therefore conduct a descriptive subsampling experiment that focuses on probes with weak marginal evidence. Specifically, we form a pool containing the 23,508 probes satisfying
|t_{j}|<2
. For each
n\in\{30,40,50,60\}
and
p\in\{40,50,60\}
, one repetition samples
n
matched pairs and
p
probes without replacement from the 60 pairs and the weak-signal pool, respectively, and then applies the four tests at level 0.05. Each configuration is repeated 10,000 times. Because the probe pool is selected from the same data, the resulting rejection frequencies are intended as a comparative sensitivity analysis rather than as estimates of type-I error or model-based power.

ERHT–CC has the largest rejection frequency in every configuration in Table
4
. The differences become more pronounced as either the number of pairs or the number of included probes increases. For example, at
(n,p)=(60,60)
, the rejection frequencies are 0.7705 for ERHT–CC, 0.6449 for RHT–CC, 0.5097 for WPL, and 0.1298 for CQ. The comparison suggests that ridge regularization of the centered SSCM is effective at accumulating many weak and correlated expression changes, while the spatial construction yields an additional gain over its covariance-based RHT–CC counterpart in this data set.

6
Conclusion

We proposed ERHT–CC for one-sample high-dimensional location testing under elliptically symmetric distributions with heavy tails and pervasive dependence. The procedure combines the sample spatial median, a centered SSCM, ridge regularization, and fixed-grid Cauchy aggregation. We established feasible null calibration, characterized local power, and showed how aggregation avoids selecting an alternative-specific ridge parameter. The simulations indicate favorable performance relative to several existing dense tests when the observations are heavy tailed or the dependence contains pervasive components, and the GSE19804 analysis illustrates its sensitivity to weak, correlated expression changes.

Two extensions are of particular interest. First, the present quadratic statistic targets dense alternatives; combining it with robust maximum or thresholding components could yield a sparsity-adaptive procedure under pervasive dependence, building on the sparse and max–sum testing ideas of
Zhong et al. 2013
,
Cai et al. 2014
,
Chen et al. 2019
,
Zhang and Feng 2024
, and
Liu et al. 2027
. Second, extending the centered-SSCM regularization and Cauchy aggregation framework to two-sample and multi-sample location problems would complement the spatial-sign methods of
Feng et al. 2016
and
Huang et al. 2023
.

Appendix A
Elliptical angular decomposition and population matrices

Throughout the appendices, all constants denoted by
C
may change from line to line and depend only on the fixed constants in Assumption 1. Every inverse-distance weight below is the elliptical weight
w_{i}=\xi_{i}(h_{i,p}/q_{i,p})^{1/2}
from (2.2); no argument uses independence between
w_{i}
and the spatial sign. Write

\mathbf{P}_{F,p}^{\top}\bm{G}_{i}=(G_{1i},\ldots,G_{ri})^{\top},\qquad\mathbf{P}_{B,p}^{\top}\bm{G}_{i}=\bm{G}_{B,i}.

Define

Q_{i,p}^{0}=\mu_{0,p}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\tau_{a,p}G_{ai}^{2},\qquad D_{i,p}^{0}=(Q_{i,p}^{0})^{-1}.

(A.1)

The two Gaussian quadratic forms in Section 2 satisfy

\displaystyle q_{i,p}

\displaystyle=Q_{i,p}^{0}+u_{i,p},

\displaystyle u_{i,p}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\lambda_{j,p}(G_{ji}^{2}-1),

\displaystyle h_{i,p}

\displaystyle=1+v_{i,p},

\displaystyle v_{i,p}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}(G_{ji}^{2}-1).

Consequently,

\ell_{i,p}=\left(\frac{1+v_{i,p}}{Q_{i,p}^{0}+u_{i,p}}\right)^{1/2}.

In the eigenbasis of
\bm{\Omega}_{p}
, the coordinates of

\bm{Y}_{i}=\sqrt{p}\,\bm{U}_{i}
are

(\mathbf{P}_{F,p}^{\top}\bm{Y}_{i})_{a}=\frac{\sqrt{p\tau_{a,p}}G_{ai}}{q_{i,p}^{1/2}},\quad 1\leq a\leq r,\qquad(\mathbf{P}_{B,p}^{\top}\bm{Y}_{i})_{j}=\frac{\lambda_{j,p}^{1/2}G_{ji}}{q_{i,p}^{1/2}},\quad r<j\leq p.

Lemma A.1
.

For every fixed integer
2\leq k\leq 8
,

\mathbb{E}|u_{i,p}|^{k}+\mathbb{E}|v_{i,p}|^{k}\leq C_{k}p^{-k/2}.

Moreover,

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\{|u_{i,p}|+|v_{i,p}|\}=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right),

(A.2)

and

\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left|\ell_{i,p}-(D_{i,p}^{0})^{1/2}\right|=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right).

(A.3)

For all
i
and all sufficiently large
p
,

0<\ell_{i,p}\leq\omega_{-}^{-1/2},\qquad 0<w_{i}\leq\bar{\xi}\omega_{-}^{-1/2}=\bar{w}.

(A.4)

Proof.

For
j>r
, put

X_{ji}=p^{-1}\lambda_{j,p}(G_{ji}^{2}-1).

The variables
X_{ji}
are independent and centered, and

\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\mathbb{E}X_{ji}^{2}=\frac{2}{p^{2}}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\lambda_{j,p}^{2}\leq\frac{2\omega_{+}^{2}}{p}.

For
k\geq 2
,

\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\mathbb{E}|X_{ji}|^{k}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{p^{k}}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\lambda_{j,p}^{k}\leq C_{k}p^{1-k}.

Rosenthal’s inequality
(
Boucheron et al. 2013
, Theorem 15.11, pp. 425–426)
therefore yields

\mathbb{E}|u_{i,p}|^{k}\leq C_{k}\left[\left\{\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\mathbb{E}X_{ji}^{2}\right\}^{k/2}+\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\mathbb{E}|X_{ji}|^{k}\right]\leq C_{k}\{p^{-k/2}+p^{1-k}\}\leq C_{k}p^{-k/2}.

The same calculation with
\lambda_{j,p}=1
gives

\mathbb{E}|v_{i,p}|^{k}\leq C_{k}p^{-k/2}
.

The centered variable
G^{2}-1
is sub-exponential. Scalar Bernstein inequalities
(
Boucheron et al. 2013
, Theorem 2.10 and Corollary 2.11, pp. 36–37)
give, for
0<t\leq 1
,

\mathbb{P}(|u_{i,p}|>t)+\mathbb{P}(|v_{i,p}|>t)\leq 4\exp(-cpt^{2}).

Taking
t=M\sqrt{\log p/p}
and using
n\leq p/c_{-}
gives

\mathbb{P}\left[\max_{i\leq n}\{|u_{i,p}|+|v_{i,p}|\}>2t\right]\leq\frac{4}{c_{-}}p^{1-cM^{2}}.

A sufficiently large fixed
M
proves (
A.2
).

On the event

\max_{i}|u_{i,p}|\leq\mu_{-}/2,\qquad\max_{i}|v_{i,p}|\leq 1/2,

the function
g(x,y)=\{(1+y)/(Q+x)\}^{1/2}
has uniformly bounded first derivatives for
Q\geq\mu_{-}
. The mean-value theorem gives

\left|\left\{\frac{1+v_{i,p}}{Q_{i,p}^{0}+u_{i,p}}\right\}^{1/2}-(Q_{i,p}^{0})^{-1/2}\right|\leq C(|u_{i,p}|+|v_{i,p}|),

which proves (
A.3
).

Finally, with
\bm{V}_{i}=\bm{G}_{i}/\|\bm{G}_{i}\|
,

\ell_{i,p}=(\bm{V}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\Omega}_{p}\bm{V}_{i})^{-1/2}.

Since
\lambda_{\min}(\bm{\Omega}_{p})\geq\omega_{-}
for all sufficiently large
p
,

\ell_{i,p}\leq\omega_{-}^{-1/2}
. Multiplication by

0<\xi_{i}\leq\bar{\xi}
proves (
A.4
).
∎

The population spatial-sign shape and the population derivative of the scaled spatial score are

\mathbf{R}_{p}=\mathbb{E}(\bm{Y}_{i}\bm{Y}_{i}^{\top}),\qquad\mathbf{J}_{E,p}=\mathbb{E}\{\ell_{i,p}(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\bm{U}_{i}\bm{U}_{i}^{\top})\}.

Because
\xi_{i}
is independent of
\bm{G}_{i}
, the derivative of

\mathbb{E}\{\sqrt{p}\,\mathbf{U}(\bm{X}_{i}-\bm{t})\}
at

\bm{t}=\bm{\theta}_{p}
equals
-m_{1}\mathbf{J}_{E,p}
.

For
Q_{p}^{0}=\mu_{0,p}+\sum_{a=1}^{r}\tau_{a,p}G_{a}^{2}
, define

\displaystyle a_{a,p}

\displaystyle=\tau_{a,p}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{G_{a}^{2}}{Q_{p}^{0}}\right),

\displaystyle b_{0,p}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\{(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1}\},

\displaystyle c_{a,p}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Q_{p}^{0}-\tau_{a,p}G_{a}^{2}}{(Q_{p}^{0})^{3/2}}\right],

\displaystyle s_{1,p}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\{(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1/2}\}.

Proposition A.1
.

Under Assumption 1,
\mathbf{R}_{p}
,
\mathbf{J}_{E,p}
, and

\bm{\Omega}_{p}
have the same eigenvectors. Uniformly in
1\leq a\leq r
and
r<j\leq p
,

\displaystyle p^{-1}\lambda_{a}(\mathbf{R}_{p})

\displaystyle=a_{a,p}+O(p^{-1/2}),

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{p})

\displaystyle=b_{0,p}\lambda_{j,p}+O(p^{-1/2}),

(A.5)

\displaystyle\lambda_{a}(\mathbf{J}_{E,p})

\displaystyle=c_{a,p}+O(p^{-1/2}),

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}(\mathbf{J}_{E,p})

\displaystyle=s_{1,p}+O(p^{-1}).

(A.6)

Furthermore,

\sum_{a=1}^{r}a_{a,p}+\mu_{0,p}b_{0,p}=1,

(A.7)

and there are constants
0<j_{-}<j_{+}<\infty
such that

j_{-}\leq\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{J}_{E,p})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{J}_{E,p})\leq j_{+},\qquad\|\mathbf{J}_{E,p}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq j_{-}^{-1}.

(A.8)

On the bulk subspace,

\left\|\mathbf{P}_{B,p}^{\top}\{\mathbf{J}_{E,p}^{-1}-s_{1,p}^{-1}\mathbf{I}_{p}\}\mathbf{P}_{B,p}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O(p^{-1}).

(A.9)

Proof.

In the eigenbasis of
\bm{\Omega}_{p}
,

\displaystyle Y_{ai}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{p\tau_{a,p}}G_{ai}}{q_{i,p}^{1/2}},

\displaystyle 1\leq a\leq r,

\displaystyle Y_{ji}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{j,p}^{1/2}G_{ji}}{q_{i,p}^{1/2}},

\displaystyle r<j\leq p.

Every denominator is an even function of every Gaussian coordinate. Changing the sign of one coordinate shows that every off-diagonal entry of
\mathbf{R}_{p}
is zero in this basis. The same argument applies to
\mathbf{J}_{E,p}
.

For
a\leq r
,

p^{-1}\lambda_{a}(\mathbf{R}_{p})=\tau_{a,p}\mathbb{E}(G_{a}^{2}/q_{p}).

On
|u_{p}|\leq\mu_{-}/2
,

|q_{p}^{-1}-(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1}|\leq C|u_{p}|.

The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and Lemma
A.1
give

\mathbb{E}\{G_{a}^{2}|q_{p}^{-1}-(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1}|;|u_{p}|\leq\mu_{-}/2\}\leq C(\mathbb{E}G_{a}^{4})^{1/2}(\mathbb{E}u_{p}^{2})^{1/2}\leq Cp^{-1/2}.

The complement has probability
O(p^{-4})
by the eighth-moment bound. Hölder’s inequality and the inverse moments of a chi-square variable give the same
O(p^{-1/2})
order there. Thus

p^{-1}\lambda_{a}(\mathbf{R}_{p})=\tau_{a,p}\mathbb{E}\{G_{a}^{2}/Q_{p}^{0}\}+O(p^{-1/2}).

For
j>r
,
G_{j}
is independent of
Q_{p}^{0}
, and the same calculation gives

\lambda_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{p})=\lambda_{j,p}\mathbb{E}(G_{j}^{2}/q_{p})=\lambda_{j,p}\mathbb{E}\{(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1}\}+O(p^{-1/2}).

This proves (
A.5
).

The factor-direction eigenvalue of
\mathbf{J}_{E,p}
is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{\ell_{p}(1-U_{a}^{2})\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{h_{p}^{1/2}\{q_{p}-\tau_{a,p}G_{a}^{2}\}}{q_{p}^{3/2}}\right].

The function

g(h,q,z)=h^{1/2}(q-z)q^{-3/2}
has polynomially bounded derivatives on

h\in[1/2,3/2]
and
q\geq\mu_{-}/2
. Lemma
A.1
, the Gaussian moments of
G_{a}
, and the same complement argument as above imply

\mathbb{E}\{\ell_{p}(1-U_{a}^{2})\}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Q_{p}^{0}-\tau_{a,p}G_{a}^{2}}{(Q_{p}^{0})^{3/2}}\right]+O(p^{-1/2}).

For
j>r
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\{\ell_{p}(1-U_{j}^{2})\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\ell_{p}-\frac{\lambda_{j,p}}{p}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{h_{p}^{1/2}G_{j}^{2}}{q_{p}^{3/2}}\right).

The second term is
O(p^{-1})
because

\lambda_{j,p}\leq\omega_{+}
and
q_{p}\geq\omega_{-}h_{p}
. In addition, Lemma
A.1
first gives

\mathbb{E}\ell_{p}=s_{1,p}+O(p^{-1/2})
. To obtain the sharper order, on

\{|v_{p}|\leq 1/2,\ |u_{p}|\leq\mu_{-}/2\}
, a second-order Taylor expansion of

f(h,q)=h^{1/2}q^{-1/2}
around
(1,Q_{p}^{0})
gives

\ell_{p}=(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1/2}+\frac{1}{2}(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1/2}v_{p}-\frac{1}{2}(Q_{p}^{0})^{-3/2}u_{p}+\mathcal{R}_{p},\qquad|\mathcal{R}_{p}|\leq C(v_{p}^{2}+u_{p}^{2}).

The complement contributes
O(p^{-1})
by the eighth-moment bounds and
Hölder’s inequality. Since
u_{p}
is a centered function of the bulk
Gaussian coordinates and is independent of
Q_{p}^{0}
,

\mathbb{E}\{(Q_{p}^{0})^{-3/2}u_{p}\}=0.

Writing

v_{p}=p^{-1}\sum_{a=1}^{r}(G_{a}^{2}-1)+p^{-1}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}(G_{j}^{2}-1),

the bulk sum is centered and independent of
Q_{p}^{0}
, while
r
is fixed and

Q_{p}^{0}\geq\mu_{-}
. Consequently,

\left|\mathbb{E}\{(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1/2}v_{p}\}\right|\leq\frac{1}{p}\sum_{a=1}^{r}\mathbb{E}\left|(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1/2}(G_{a}^{2}-1)\right|\leq Cp^{-1}.

Moreover,
\mathbb{E}(v_{p}^{2}+u_{p}^{2})\leq Cp^{-1}
. Therefore

\mathbb{E}\ell_{p}=s_{1,p}+O(p^{-1})
, and the bulk relation in
(
A.6
) follows.

The identity (
A.7
) is immediate from

\sum_{a=1}^{r}a_{a,p}+\mu_{0,p}b_{0,p}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\sum_{a=1}^{r}\tau_{a,p}G_{a}^{2}+\mu_{0,p}}{Q_{p}^{0}}\right]=1.

Since
Q_{p}^{0}-\tau_{a,p}G_{a}^{2}\geq\mu_{0,p}\geq\mu_{-}
,

c_{a,p}\geq\mu_{-}\mathbb{E}\{(Q_{p}^{0})^{-3/2}\}\geq\mu_{-}(\mathbb{E}Q_{p}^{0})^{-3/2}=\mu_{-},

where Jensen’s inequality was used and
\mathbb{E}Q_{p}^{0}=1
. Similarly,

s_{1,p}\geq(\mathbb{E}Q_{p}^{0})^{-1/2}=1
. Upper bounds follow from

Q_{p}^{0}\geq\mu_{-}
. Equations (
A.6
) then prove
(
A.8
). Finally, on the bulk subspace,

\mathbf{P}_{B,p}^{\top}\mathbf{J}_{E,p}\mathbf{P}_{B,p}=s_{1,p}\mathbf{I}_{p-r}+\mathbf{E}_{B,p},\qquad\|\mathbf{E}_{B,p}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O(p^{-1}).

The inverse identity

(s\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E})^{-1}-s^{-1}\mathbf{I}=-s^{-1}(s\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{E})^{-1}\mathbf{E}

and
s_{1,p}\geq 1
prove (
A.9
).
∎

Corollary A.2
.

For

\bm{\Omega}_{p}=(1-\varrho)\mathbf{I}_{p}+\varrho\mathbf{1}_{p}\mathbf{1}_{p}^{\top},\qquad 0<\varrho<1,

Assumption 1(iii)–(iv) holds with
r=1
,

\tau_{1,p}=\varrho+(1-\varrho)/p\to\varrho
,

H_{B}=\delta_{1-\varrho}
, and

Q^{0}=1-\varrho+\varrho G^{2},\qquad D^{0}=(1-\varrho+\varrho G^{2})^{-1}.

In particular,

\displaystyle p^{-1}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{R}_{p})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{\varrho G^{2}}{1-\varrho+\varrho G^{2}}\right\},

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}(\mathbf{R}_{p})

\displaystyle\to(1-\varrho)\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{1}{1-\varrho+\varrho G^{2}}\right\},\qquad j\geq 2,

and the factor-direction eigenvalue of
\mathbf{J}_{E,p}
converges to

(1-\varrho)\mathbb{E}(1-\varrho+\varrho G^{2})^{-3/2}>0.

Proof.

The vector
p^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{p}
is an eigenvector with eigenvalue

1+(p-1)\varrho
, and every orthogonal vector has eigenvalue

1-\varrho
. Substitution into Proposition
A.1

proves all assertions.
∎

Appendix B
Separable deterministic equivalents and weighted companion functionals

Put

c_{B,n}=\frac{p-r}{n}.

The use of
c_{B,n}
, rather than
c_{n}=p/n
, is important at finite
p
because the separable proxy below has
p-r
rows. Since
r
is fixed,
c_{B,n}-c_{n}=-r/n
and both ratios have the same limit.

Let
F_{D,p}
be the distribution of
D_{p}^{0}=(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1}
, with
Q_{p}^{0}
defined in (
A.1
). For every
\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
, let
(\delta_{p,\rho},\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho})
be the unique positive solution of

\displaystyle\delta_{p,\rho}

\displaystyle=c_{B,n}\int\frac{t}{\rho(1+\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}t)}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B,p}(t),

(B.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d}{\rho(1+\delta_{p,\rho}d)}\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D,p}(d).

(B.2)

Existence and uniqueness follow from the canonical-equation construction in Theorem 2.4 of
Hachem et al. 2007
. Set

\displaystyle\lambda_{p,\rho}(d)

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{p,\rho}d}{1+\delta_{p,\rho}d},

\displaystyle\kappa_{p,\rho}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\int\lambda_{p,\rho}(d)\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D,p}(d),

\displaystyle s_{a,p}

\displaystyle=\int d^{a/2}\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D,p}(d),

\displaystyle b_{a,p,\rho}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\int d^{a/2}\lambda_{p,\rho}(d)\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D,p}(d),\qquad a=1,2,

and

\displaystyle u_{p,\rho}^{\star}

\displaystyle=c_{B,n}\int\frac{t^{2}}{(1+\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}t)^{2}}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B,p}(t),

\displaystyle v_{p,\rho}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{2}}{(1+\delta_{p,\rho}d)^{2}}\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D,p}(d),

\displaystyle A_{a,p,\rho}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{1+a/2}}{(1+\delta_{p,\rho}d)^{2}}\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D,p}(d),\qquad a=0,1,2.

Lemma B.1
.

There are constants
0<c_{\delta}<C_{\delta}<\infty
and
s_{0}>0
, depending only on the constants in Assumption 1, such that, for all sufficiently large
p
and all
\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
,

c_{\delta}\leq\delta_{p,\rho},\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}\leq C_{\delta}

(B.3)

and

\rho^{2}-u_{p,\rho}^{\star}v_{p,\rho}^{\star}\geq s_{0}.

(B.4)

Proof.

Since
\omega_{-}\leq t\leq\omega_{+}
and
0<d\leq d_{+}:=\mu_{-}^{-1}
,

\delta_{p,\rho}\leq\frac{c_{+}\omega_{+}}{\rho_{0}},\qquad\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}\leq\frac{d_{+}}{\rho_{0}}.

Moreover,
\mathbb{E}Q_{p}^{0}=1
and the convexity of
x\mapsto x^{-1}
give

\int d\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D,p}(d)=\mathbb{E}(Q_{p}^{0})^{-1}\geq 1
. Hence

\delta_{p,\rho}\geq\frac{(c_{-}/2)\omega_{-}}{\rho_{1}\{1+(d_{+}/\rho_{0})\omega_{+}\}},\qquad\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}\geq\frac{1}{\rho_{1}\{1+(c_{+}\omega_{+}/\rho_{0})d_{+}\}},

where
c_{B,n}\geq c_{-}/2
for all sufficiently large
p
. This proves (
B.3
).

Put

\alpha_{p,\rho}=\frac{\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}\omega_{+}}{1+\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}\omega_{+}},\qquad\beta_{p,\rho}=\frac{\delta_{p,\rho}d_{+}}{1+\delta_{p,\rho}d_{+}}.

Both quantities are uniformly smaller than one by (
B.3
). The first canonical equation gives

\displaystyle u_{p,\rho}^{\star}

\displaystyle=c_{B,n}\int\frac{t}{1+\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}t}\frac{t}{1+\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}t}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B,p}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha_{p,\rho}}{\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}}c_{B,n}\int\frac{t}{1+\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}t}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B,p}(t)=\rho\delta_{p,\rho}\frac{\alpha_{p,\rho}}{\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}}.

Similarly,

v_{p,\rho}^{\star}\leq\rho\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}\frac{\beta_{p,\rho}}{\delta_{p,\rho}}.

Therefore

u_{p,\rho}^{\star}v_{p,\rho}^{\star}\leq\rho^{2}\alpha_{p,\rho}\beta_{p,\rho}\leq\rho^{2}(1-\varepsilon_{0})

for a fixed
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. Since
\rho\geq\rho_{0}
, (
B.4
) follows with
s_{0}=\rho_{0}^{2}\varepsilon_{0}
.
∎

For
a,b\in\{0,1,2\}
and
m_{0}=1
, define

\Psi_{ab,p}^{\star}(\rho)=m_{a}m_{b}\frac{u_{p,\rho}^{\star}A_{a,p,\rho}^{\star}A_{b,p,\rho}^{\star}}{\rho^{2}-u_{p,\rho}^{\star}v_{p,\rho}^{\star}}.

Also put

D_{p,\rho}^{\star}=m_{1}^{2}(s_{1,p}-b_{1,p,\rho}^{\star})^{2}+m_{2}\kappa_{p,\rho}^{\star}(s_{2,p}-b_{2,p,\rho}^{\star}).

Let
\bm{\Gamma}_{p,\rho}^{\star}
have the same form as
(2.4), with
\psi_{ab,n}
replaced by

\Psi_{ab,p}^{\star}(\rho)
, and define

\bm{g}_{p,\rho}^{\star}=(D_{p,\rho}^{\star})^{-2}\begin{pmatrix}m_{1}^{2}(s_{1,p}-b_{1,p,\rho}^{\star})^{2}\\
2\kappa_{p,\rho}^{\star}m_{1}(s_{1,p}-b_{1,p,\rho}^{\star})\\
(\kappa_{p,\rho}^{\star})^{2}\end{pmatrix},\qquad(\sigma_{E,p}^{\star})^{2}=(\bm{g}_{p,\rho}^{\star})^{\top}\bm{\Gamma}_{p,\rho}^{\star}\bm{g}_{p,\rho}^{\star}.

(B.5)

The next lemma supplies the first- and second-order resolvent statements used below. Its proof records the empirical fixed-point step that is needed when the column scales
D_{i,p}^{0}
are random.

Lemma B.2
.

Condition on
\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0}=\operatorname{diag}(D_{1,p}^{0},\ldots,D_{n,p}^{0})
and let

(\delta_{n,\rho}^{D},\widetilde{\delta}_{n,\rho}^{D})
be the positive solution of

\displaystyle\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}

\displaystyle=c_{B,n}\int\frac{t}{\rho(1+\widetilde{\delta}_{n,\rho}^{D}t)}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B,p}(t),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}_{n,\rho}^{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{D_{i,p}^{0}}{\rho(1+\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0})}.

For every fixed
\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
,

|\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}-\delta_{p,\rho}|+|\widetilde{\delta}_{n,\rho}^{D}-\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

(B.6)

Let

\overline{\mathbf{B}}_{B,n}=\frac{1}{n}\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{B}\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0}\mathbf{G}_{B}^{\top}\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}^{1/2},\qquad\overline{\mathbf{R}}_{n}=(\overline{\mathbf{B}}_{B,n}+\rho\mathbf{I}_{p-r})^{-1},

and let
\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n}
be the companion matrix in
(
B.19
). Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{tr}\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n}-\kappa_{p,\rho}^{\star}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}),

(B.7)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n})_{ii}-\lambda_{p,\rho}(D_{i,p}^{0})\}^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1}),

(B.8)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\overline{\mathbf{R}}_{n}\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\overline{\mathbf{R}}_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{p,\rho}^{\star}}{\rho^{2}-u_{p,\rho}^{\star}v_{p,\rho}^{\star}}+O_{\mathbb{P}}(p^{-1/4}).

(B.9)

The same last relation holds for the resolvent obtained by deleting any fixed number not exceeding two of the columns, with an additional
O(p^{-1})
error.

Proof.

For fixed
x
in the compact interval in (
B.3
), the variables

\psi_{x}(D_{i,p}^{0})=\frac{D_{i,p}^{0}}{\rho(1+xD_{i,p}^{0})}

are independent, bounded by
(\rho_{0}\mu_{-})^{-1}
, and have a Lipschitz constant at most
\rho_{0}^{-1}\mu_{-}^{-2}
in
x
. Bernstein’s inequality
(
Boucheron et al. 2013
, Theorem 2.10 and Corollary 2.11, pp. 36–37)
on a grid of mesh
n^{-2}
, followed by the Lipschitz extension from the grid, gives

\sup_{0\leq x\leq C_{\delta}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{x}(D_{i,p}^{0})-\mathbb{E}\psi_{x}(D_{p}^{0})\right|=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}\right).

(B.10)

We first obtain consistency without linearizing at an unverified intermediate point. The same elementary bounds used in (
B.3
), with the empirical average in place of expectation, show that both empirical coordinates belong to a fixed compact interval with probability tending to one. We now compare the empirical and finite-
p
deterministic solutions through their common subsequential limits. Take an arbitrary subsequence. Compactness permits a further subsequence along which

(\delta_{p,\rho},\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho})\to(\delta_{\star},\widetilde{\delta}_{\star}).

Along a still further subsequence, the left-hand side of
(
B.10
) converges to zero almost surely. Assumption 1, the continuous-mapping theorem, and
c_{B,n}\to c
give

H_{B,p}\Rightarrow H_{B},\qquad F_{D,p}\Rightarrow F_{D}.

For every outcome in this probability-one event, any convergent subsubsequence of

(\delta_{n,\rho}^{D},\widetilde{\delta}_{n,\rho}^{D})
therefore satisfies

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=c\int\frac{t}{\rho(1+\widetilde{\delta}t)}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B}(t),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d}{\rho(1+\delta d)}\,\,\mathrm{d}F_{D}(d).

The deterministic limit
(\delta_{\star},\widetilde{\delta}_{\star})
satisfies the same system. Its positive solution is unique, so the two limits coincide. Thus every subsequence has a further subsequence on which

|\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}-\delta_{p,\rho}|+|\widetilde{\delta}_{n,\rho}^{D}-\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}|\to 0

almost surely. The subsequence criterion yields

|\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}-\delta_{p,\rho}|+|\widetilde{\delta}_{n,\rho}^{D}-\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

At the deterministic point
\delta_{p,\rho}
, Bernstein’s inequality
(
Boucheron et al. 2013
, Theorem 2.10 and Corollary 2.11, pp. 36–37)
gives

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{\delta_{p,\rho}}(D_{i,p}^{0})-\mathbb{E}\psi_{\delta_{p,\rho}}(D_{p}^{0})=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

Subtracting the empirical and population systems and applying the mean-value theorem now gives

\begin{pmatrix}1&u_{n,\rho}^{D}/\rho\\
v_{n,\rho}^{D}/\rho&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}-\delta_{p,\rho}\\
\widetilde{\delta}_{n,\rho}^{D}-\widetilde{\delta}_{p,\rho}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2})\end{pmatrix},

where
u_{n,\rho}^{D}
and
v_{n,\rho}^{D}
are evaluated on the line segment joining the two solutions. The preceding
o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
relation and continuity of the derivatives imply

\rho^{2}-u_{n,\rho}^{D}v_{n,\rho}^{D}=\rho^{2}-u_{p,\rho}^{\star}v_{p,\rho}^{\star}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)\geq s_{0}/2

with probability tending to one. The inverse of the displayed
2\times 2
matrix is therefore
O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
, proving (
B.6
).

Conditional on
\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0}
, the matrix

\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{B}(\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0})^{1/2}/\sqrt{n}
has a bounded separable variance profile. Theorem 1.1 of

Hachem et al. 2013
, applied to the deterministic
coordinate vectors
\bm{e}_{i}
, supplies a uniform second-moment bound for
each diagonal bilinear form. In the present notation this gives

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{G}_{B}}\left[\left.\left\{(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n})_{ii}-\frac{\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0}}{1+\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0}}\right\}^{2}\right|\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0}\right]\leq\frac{C}{n},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n.

Averaging this inequality and applying conditional Markov’s inequality,
and then applying Cauchy–Schwarz to the averaged diagonal error, yield

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n})_{ii}-\frac{\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0}}{1+\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0}}\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1}),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{tr}\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0}}{1+\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0}}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

The Lipschitz bound

\left|\frac{xd}{1+xd}-\frac{yd}{1+yd}\right|\leq d_{+}|x-y|

and (
B.6
), followed by Bernstein’s inequality
(
Boucheron et al. 2013
, Theorem 2.10 and Corollary 2.11, pp. 36–37)
for the final empirical average, prove
(
B.7
)–(
B.8
). Notice that Theorem 2.4 of
Hachem et al. 2007
is used only for existence and uniqueness of the deterministic system; the bilinear moment bound needed here is Theorem 1.1 of
Hachem et al. 2013
.

It remains to prove (
B.9
). For
0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, define

\overline{\mathbf{R}}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\{\overline{\mathbf{B}}_{B,n}+\rho\mathbf{I}_{p-r}+\varepsilon\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\}^{-1},\qquad\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{tr}\{\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\overline{\mathbf{R}}_{n}(\varepsilon)\}.

Direct differentiation gives

\displaystyle-\widehat{\delta}_{n}^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\overline{\mathbf{R}}_{n}\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\overline{\mathbf{R}}_{n}),

\displaystyle|\widehat{\delta}_{n}^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}^{3})\rho_{0}^{-3}\leq C.

Let
h_{n}=n^{-1/4}
. Taylor’s formula therefore gives

-\widehat{\delta}_{n}^{\prime}(0)=-\frac{\widehat{\delta}_{n}(h_{n})-\widehat{\delta}_{n}(0)}{h_{n}}+O(h_{n}).

(B.11)

For
\varepsilon>0
, premultiplication and postmultiplication by

(\rho\mathbf{I}_{p-r}+\varepsilon\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p})^{-1/2}
reduce the matrix
again to the separable covariance model with row scales

\lambda_{j,p}/(\rho+\varepsilon\lambda_{j,p})
and scalar ridge one.
The bilinear resolvent bound just used for
(
B.7
), applied after this rescaling at

\varepsilon=0
and
\varepsilon=h_{n}
, gives

\widehat{\delta}_{n}(\varepsilon)-\delta_{n}^{D}(\varepsilon)=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}),\qquad\varepsilon\in\{0,h_{n}\},

where
\delta_{n}^{D}(\varepsilon)
is the first component of the perturbed empirical canonical system

\displaystyle\delta_{n}^{D}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=r+1}^{p}\frac{\lambda_{j,p}}{\rho+\varepsilon\lambda_{j,p}+\rho\widetilde{\delta}_{n}^{D}(\varepsilon)\lambda_{j,p}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}_{n}^{D}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{D_{i,p}^{0}}{\rho(1+\delta_{n}^{D}(\varepsilon)D_{i,p}^{0})}.

All first two derivatives of this system are uniformly bounded by
Lemma
B.1
. Differentiating at zero gives

\displaystyle\{\delta_{n}^{D}\}^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{u_{n,\rho}^{D}}{\rho^{2}-u_{n,\rho}^{D}v_{n,\rho}^{D}},

\displaystyle u_{n,\rho}^{D}

\displaystyle=c_{B,n}\int\frac{t^{2}}{(1+\widetilde{\delta}_{n,\rho}^{D}t)^{2}}\,\,\mathrm{d}H_{B,p}(t),

\displaystyle v_{n,\rho}^{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(D_{i,p}^{0})^{2}}{(1+\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0})^{2}}.

Consequently,

-\frac{\delta_{n}^{D}(h_{n})-\delta_{n}^{D}(0)}{h_{n}}=\frac{u_{n,\rho}^{D}}{\rho^{2}-u_{n,\rho}^{D}v_{n,\rho}^{D}}+O(h_{n}).

(B.12)

Equations (
B.6
) and Bernstein’s inequality
(
Boucheron et al. 2013
, Theorem 2.10 and Corollary 2.11, pp. 36–37)
imply

|u_{n,\rho}^{D}-u_{p,\rho}^{\star}|+|v_{n,\rho}^{D}-v_{p,\rho}^{\star}|=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

Substitution into (
B.11
)–(
B.12
) yields

-\widehat{\delta}_{n}^{\prime}(0)=\frac{u_{p,\rho}^{\star}}{\rho^{2}-u_{p,\rho}^{\star}v_{p,\rho}^{\star}}+O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}h_{n}^{-1}+h_{n})=\frac{u_{p,\rho}^{\star}}{\rho^{2}-u_{p,\rho}^{\star}v_{p,\rho}^{\star}}+O_{\mathbb{P}}(p^{-1/4}).

Deleting one or two columns is a fixed-rank perturbation. Applying the resolvent identity before the normalized trace changes the last display by at most
C/n
, which proves the final assertion.
∎

Proposition B.1
.

Under Assumption 1, for every fixed

\rho\in[\rho_{0},\rho_{1}]
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{n}-\kappa_{p,\rho}^{\star}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right),

(B.13)

\displaystyle e_{n}-m_{1}s_{1,p}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right),

\displaystyle t_{n}-m_{2}s_{2,p}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right),

(B.14)

\displaystyle b_{a,n}-m_{a}b_{a,p,\rho}^{\star}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right),\qquad a=1,2,

(B.15)

\displaystyle\psi_{ab,n}-\Psi_{ab,p}^{\star}(\rho)

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}(p^{-1/4}),\qquad a,b\in\{0,1,2\}.

(B.16)

Consequently,

D_{n}-D_{p,\rho}^{\star}=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right),\qquad\sigma_{D,n}^{2}-(\sigma_{E,p}^{\star})^{2}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(p^{-1/4}).

(B.17)

There are constants
0<C_{1}<C_{2}<\infty
such that, with probability tending to one,

C_{1}\leq D_{n},\ \mu_{n},\ \sigma_{D,n}^{2}\leq C_{2}.

(B.18)

Proof.

Let

\mathbf{Y}_{B}=\mathbf{P}_{B,p}^{\top}\mathbf{Y},\qquad\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{B}=\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}^{1/2}\mathbf{G}_{B}(\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0})^{1/2},\qquad\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0}=\operatorname{diag}(D_{1,p}^{0},\ldots,D_{n,p}^{0}).

Define

\overline{\mathbf{B}}_{B,n}=n^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{B}\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{B}^{\top},\qquad\overline{\mathbf{K}}_{B,n}=n^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{B}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{Y}}_{B},

(B.19)

and put

\mathbf{K}_{B,n}=n^{-1}\mathbf{Y}_{B}^{\top}\mathbf{Y}_{B},\qquad\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n}=\overline{\mathbf{K}}_{B,n}(\overline{\mathbf{K}}_{B,n}+\rho\mathbf{I}_{n})^{-1}.

Lemma
A.1
and

\|\mathbf{G}_{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{p})
give

\|\mathbf{K}_{B,n}-\overline{\mathbf{K}}_{B,n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right).

For positive semidefinite
\mathbf{K}_{1},\mathbf{K}_{2}
,

\mathbf{K}_{1}(\mathbf{K}_{1}+\rho\mathbf{I})^{-1}-\mathbf{K}_{2}(\mathbf{K}_{2}+\rho\mathbf{I})^{-1}=\rho(\mathbf{K}_{2}+\rho\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\mathbf{K}_{1}-\mathbf{K}_{2})(\mathbf{K}_{1}+\rho\mathbf{I})^{-1}.

Thus, with
\mathbf{A}_{B,n}=\mathbf{K}_{B,n}(\mathbf{K}_{B,n}+\rho\mathbf{I})^{-1}
,

\|\mathbf{A}_{B,n}-\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right).

(B.20)

The full Gram matrix differs from the bulk Gram matrix by a positive semidefinite matrix of rank at most
r
. Resolvent monotonicity and eigenvalue interlacing give

\operatorname{rank}(\mathbf{A}_{n}-\mathbf{A}_{B,n})\leq r,\qquad\|\mathbf{A}_{n}-\mathbf{A}_{B,n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1,\qquad\left|n^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{n}-\mathbf{A}_{B,n})\right|\leq r/n.

(B.21)

Lemma
B.2
now proves (
B.13
).

Put
\widetilde{w}_{i}=\xi_{i}(D_{i,p}^{0})^{1/2}
. By
(
A.3
),

\max_{i\leq n}|w_{i}-\widetilde{w}_{i}|=O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right).

(B.22)

For each
a=1,2
, the variables
\widetilde{w}_{i}^{a}
are independent across
i
and uniformly bounded. Hence

n^{-1}\sum_{i}\widetilde{w}_{i}^{a}-m_{a}s_{a,p}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}),

which proves (
B.14
).

For
a=1,2
, decompose

\displaystyle b_{a,n}-m_{a}b_{a,p,\rho}^{\star}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}(\mathbf{A}_{n})_{ii}\{w_{i}^{a}-\widetilde{w}_{i}^{a}\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{tr}\{\mathbf{D}_{\widetilde{w}}^{a}(\mathbf{A}_{B,n}-\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n})\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{tr}\{\mathbf{D}_{\widetilde{w}}^{a}(\mathbf{A}_{n}-\mathbf{A}_{B,n})\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\widetilde{w}_{i}^{a}\{(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n})_{ii}-\lambda_{p,\rho}(D_{i,p}^{0})\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\left[\xi_{i}^{a}(D_{i,p}^{0})^{a/2}\lambda_{p,\rho}(D_{i,p}^{0})-m_{a}b_{a,p,\rho}^{\star}\right],

where
\mathbf{D}_{\widetilde{w}}=\operatorname{diag}(\widetilde{w}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{w}_{n})
. The five terms are, respectively,

O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right),\quad O_{\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{p}}\right),\quad O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1}),\quad O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}),\quad O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

For the third term we used

|\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{D}_{\widetilde{w}}^{a}(\mathbf{A}_{n}-\mathbf{A}_{B,n}))|\leq\bar{w}^{a}\|\mathbf{A}_{n}-\mathbf{A}_{B,n}\|_{*}
and
(
B.21
); the fourth follows from
(
B.8
). This proves (
B.15
).

It remains to prove (
B.16
). Conditional on the angular observations, changing one radial variable
\xi_{i}
changes the off-diagonal weighted sum by at most

\frac{C}{n}\sum_{j\neq i}\{(\mathbf{A}_{n})_{ij}^{2}+(\mathbf{A}_{n})_{ji}^{2}\}\leq\frac{C}{n}.

The Efron–Stein inequality
(
Boucheron et al. 2013
, Theorem 3.1, p. 54)
therefore gives

\psi_{ab,n}-m_{a}m_{b}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq j}(\mathbf{A}_{n})_{ij}^{2}\ell_{i,p}^{a}\ell_{j,p}^{b}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

(B.23)

Replacing
\ell_{i,p}
by
(D_{i,p}^{0})^{1/2}
and
\mathbf{A}_{n}
by

\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n}
costs

O_{\mathbb{P}}(\sqrt{\log p/p})
by
(
B.22
), (
B.20
),
(
B.21
), and

n^{-1}\operatorname{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{n}^{2})\leq 1
. It remains to evaluate

T_{ab,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq j}(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n})_{ij}^{2}(D_{i,p}^{0})^{a/2}(D_{j,p}^{0})^{b/2}.

(B.24)

Conditional on
\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0}
, write

\bm{x}_{i}=(D_{i,p}^{0})^{1/2}\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}^{1/2}\bm{g}_{i}
and let

\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}=\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k\neq i,j}\bm{x}_{k}\bm{x}_{k}^{\top}+\rho\mathbf{I}_{p-r}\right)^{-1}.

The two-column Schur complement gives, for
i\neq j
,

(\overline{\mathbf{A}}_{n})_{ij}=\frac{n^{-1}\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{j}}{\{1+n^{-1}\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{i}\}\{1+n^{-1}\bm{x}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{j}\}-n^{-2}(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{j})^{2}}.

(B.25)

Theorem 1.1 of
Hachem et al. 2013
gives the averaged quadratic-form bounds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\left|n^{-1}\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(i)}\bm{x}_{i}-\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1}),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\left|n^{-1}\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{j}\right|^{4}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-2}).

Consequently, replacing the denominator in (
B.25
) by

\{1+\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0}\}\{1+\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{j,p}^{0}\}
changes
T_{ab,n}
by

O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2})
.

Put

\gamma_{i}=(1+\delta_{n,\rho}^{D}D_{i,p}^{0})^{-1},\qquad q_{ij}=\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{j},

and define

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{ab,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum_{i\neq j}(D_{i,p}^{0})^{a/2}(D_{j,p}^{0})^{b/2}\gamma_{i}^{2}\gamma_{j}^{2}q_{ij}^{2},

\displaystyle M_{ab,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{3}}\sum_{i\neq j}(D_{i,p}^{0})^{1+a/2}(D_{j,p}^{0})^{1+b/2}\gamma_{i}^{2}\gamma_{j}^{2}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}).

The preceding denominator replacement gives

T_{ab,n}-\widetilde{T}_{ab,n}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(n^{-1/2})
.
Moreover, conditional on
\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}
and
\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0}
,

\mathbb{E}_{i,j}q_{ij}^{2}=D_{i,p}^{0}D_{j,p}^{0}\operatorname{tr}(\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{\Lambda}_{B,p}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}),

so
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{G}_{B}}(\widetilde{T}_{ab,n}-M_{ab,n}\mid\mathbf{D}_{p}^{0})=0
.
We next verify concentration around this conditional expectation rather
than using the Poincaré inequality to identify the expectation itself.
For
k\notin\{i,j\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial q_{ij}}{\partial g_{\alpha k}}=-\frac{\sqrt{D_{k,p}^{0}\lambda_{B,\alpha,p}}}{n}\big\{

\displaystyle(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{e}_{\alpha})(\bm{x}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{j})

\displaystyle+(\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{k})(\bm{e}_{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{j})\big\},

whereas

\frac{\partial q_{ij}}{\partial g_{\alpha i}}=\sqrt{D_{i,p}^{0}\lambda_{B,\alpha,p}}\bm{e}_{\alpha}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{x}_{j},\qquad\frac{\partial q_{ij}}{\partial g_{\alpha j}}=\sqrt{D_{j,p}^{0}\lambda_{B,\alpha,p}}\bm{x}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{R}^{(ij)}\bm{e}_{\alpha}.

In addition, for
k\notin\{i,j\}
,
